b. Consideration and approval of a proclamation declaring September 11
through September 14, 2024, as "Navajo County Fair Days" :
Rusty Despain thanked the Supervisors for their support throughout the
years, he also thanked Supervisor Whitesinger for her involvement with
securing the bleachers, last minute. Mr. DeSpain explained some of the
administration changes to the Fair, with a new Fair Manager, Mina
Henning, and himself as the new Fair President. He thanked Michael
Sample for his presidency for the past 12 years. He reported that the
Governor’s fund did come through for this year. Mr. DeSpain read the
proclamation and asked that it be adopted. Mr. DeSpain thanked
administration for volunteering and helping throughout the fair. He stated
that this year the Fair Board presented to County Manager Layton a
five-year plan for facility upgrades and so forth.
Supervisor Whitesinger stated that the fair does not go on without
hundreds of hours of volunteer hours and she expressed her appreciation
to all of those who volunteer.
Supervisor Whitesinger made a motion to approve a proclamation
declaring September 11 through September 14, 2024, as "Navajo County
Fair Days", motion seconded by Supervisor Peshlakai. 
Chairman Whiting thanked Mr. DeSpain for all that he does, for his
leadership and willingness to come back as the President. He noted that
the County Fair is something that brings our communities together.
Vote unanimous approving the motion.

PLANNING & DEVELOPMENT SERVICES: 
 
a. Presentation regarding the ongoing update to the Navajo County
Comprehensive Plan, including upcoming dates and events as the update
moves toward the hearing process:
Cody Cooper reported on Comprehensive Plan, noting that it is a guide for
development in the County for the next 20-30 years and that they are
statutorily required to review it every 10 years.  He stated that the main
purpose is to help with conversation of resources, money usage, and the
health, safety and welfare, which is also guided through many of their
processes.  He spoke about planning hierarchy, noting that the
comprehensive plan is a strong foundational document, then the next step
would be zoning and corridor plans, then the regulator documents such as
the zoning ordinance and other ordinances in the zoning and corridor plan
phase, next is the site plans and then the general plans and certificate of
occupancy.  He stated that as far as plan contents, based on our
population, Navajo County is only required to cover two elements in the
plan as required by state law, land use and transportation, but that due to
the growth and needs, and wanting to be forward thinking, they have put in
ten topics, noting that water and wastewater are combined, but are two
topics.  He indicated that 10 topics is a lot to have the public and
developers to read through, so they have combined them into three main
themes that are like uses.  He spoke about the community questionnaire
that was put out at events and on the comprehensive plan website, noting
that they received 135 responses and indicated that the questionnaire has
been shut down so they can start to bring the ideas together to guide the
plan update.  He spoke about the responses that were received, noting that
some of the items are conflicting in nature, which is natural within this type
of planning and that there has to be some give and take with the conflicts. 
He stated that it is very important to continue encouraging community
feedback so they can refine the balance and figure out where the scales
need to be in the end, noting that this process is an organized way for
residents to share thoughts and perspective that will help to directly craft
and influence the document that will guide the county decisions on future
projects.  He stated that the document is the communities document, and
their input will guide what is adopted.  He spoke about the community
interviews that have been done with some major stakeholders, the
community events that have been held as well as the ones that are coming
up.  He stated that just through the events they have engaged with over
  
                                                                    4 of 11 
DRAFT

<!-- page 12 -->
900 community members so far.  He spoke about next steps, indicating
they will have the 60-day review as required by state law, and will have to
giving the plan to adjacent counties that physical touch Navajo County, any
of the cities within the county boundaries and the local and regionalized
agencies; and the public will be able to review the plan as well.  He stated
that they will schedule open houses during review period, noting that there
will be one October 3, 2024, from 6:00 to 8:00 p.m. at the Navajo County
Governmental Complex and another one at the Show Low City Hall,
October 4, 2024, from 11:00 a.m. to 1:00 pm.  He indicated that their hope
is to have members of the public come in so they can discuss the items
with them and get feedback.  He stated that after that the next step would
be the Planning and Zoning Commission Hearing for recommendation of
the Comprehensive Plan, which they anticipate doing on December 12,
2024, and the final event would be the Board of Supervisors Meeting for
potential adoption, on January 14, 2025. 
Supervisor Seymore thanked Mr. Cooper and the team for the efforts put
forth in preparing this.  He stated that there is a lot of work that goes into
this and that it’s the foundation for planning for the county.  He encouraged
citizens to get out and be part of the process.  He indicated that it’s a very
hard process, but that once established that they will be able to go through
every 10 years to make changes.  He indicated that it is a foundational
piece of planning that is very important to the community.   
Supervisor Whitesinger thanked Mr. Cooper and the team.  She stated
that it’s a great effort to go into community and being able to reach out to so
many members of the public to get input.  She stated that she appreciates
that they expanded outside of just the land use and transportation to get a
comprehensive perspective of how those other topics impact the
community.  She thanked them for getting the community involved.
Chairman Whiting thanked Mr. Cooper and the team for getting out to
engage with the public to make sure that their voices are being heard.  He
inquired how they will get the information out to the public for the open
houses.
Mr. Cooper spoke about the different ways that they are putting information
out, including on their website, the comprehensive plan website, doing a
press brief and working with Alicia Deets for social media blasts.
Chairman Whiting inquired if it is their intent to talk with people and get
feedback about the county’s role for topics such as renewables and cell
towers, what can be done inside their authority.
Mr. Cooper stated that they always want to engage, educate and discuss
with the public about the roles of staff, the Board of Supervisors and the
community members.  He indicated that the comprehensive plan will go
through and guide some of their organized documents and how that will
assist them prior to coming before the Board.  He indicated that cell towers,
that they do see it as a large topic, that for cell towers they are looking at
the ordinance that has been developed but that it’s largely regulated
federally through the Telecommunications Act of 1996.  He indicated that
for cell towers the health and safety aspect is not done through the county.
He indicated that the county is limited on what they can review for cell
towers, that it is generally the setbacks and height and that through federal
regulations those have to be considered reasonable, or the ordinance will
be stricken as not legal and could result in court issues.  He stated that the
                                                                    5 of 11 
DRAFT

<!-- page 13 -->
health and safety aspect is fully regulated by the Federal Communications
Commission and that the county has no oversite and is not allowed to have
oversite in those cases.
Chairman Whiting spoke about having sympathy for those citizens that do
not wanting to have near the sites near them due to health concerns but
noted that they don’t have the authorize to address that.  He inquired if the
Board could refer them to the correct agency.
Mr. Cooper talked about getting a fact sheets together to explain the
counties involvement through the process and what they can oversee and
how the public can reach out to the other agencies for the areas that each
organization oversees.
Chairman Whiting and Mr. Cooper spoke about the different agencies
involved in the different processes.
Chairman Whiting shared his appreciation to Mr. Cooper and requested
that the Board be kept informed.  He shared his appreciation for the public
outreach.  
 
COUNTY BUSINESS UPDATE: Report from County Manager, County Attorney and
Board members :
Bryan Layton, County Manager, shared his appreciation to Ken Maryama and
Sherry Reed for the agenda item on the window replacement at the old courthouse,
noting that it was a much-needed update to the historic building.  He spoke about
the comments made regarding getting feedback from the poll workers that worked
the last election, noting that there is a survey in the packet that goes to every vote
center but that there wasn’t much feedback.  He stated that a letter with a QR code
has been mailed to the three-hundred-seventy plus poll workers, that there has
been approximately thirty people that have responded, and that they hope to get
some good feedback to see if there’s area that need improvement. He spoke about
the Navajo County Fair proclamation and that the employees who are volunteering
for fair, noting that there are a lot of hours that staff put in.
Supervisor Seymore stated that they are thankful for the fair and for the people that
that give their time and get family members involved.  He encouraged citizens to be
a part of the fair.  He thanked Jayson Vowell and his team on receiving the Financial
Excellence Award, noting that it’s a consistent award that we have been receiving.
He spoke about having challenges in our life and indicated that some words that
could be helpful are focus, friends, family, faith, freedom and football. 
Supervisor Peshlakai reported on attending the Sage Memorial Hospital Grand
Opening, noting that it is in Apache County but that residents from Greasewood,
Low Mountain, and Jeddito receive services there.  He indicated that he participated
in the Southwest Tabacco Education Prevention Program, which is a nonprofit
organization located in Winslow, and spoke about the updates regarding them
advocating to secure a percentage in increasing sales tax of tobacco products on the
Navajo Nation and smoking being prohibited near public places within the Navajo
Reservation.   He shared his appreciation to staff for attending and representing
Navajo County at the 30th Anniversary for the Navajo Department of Transportation.
 He spoke about the meeting held in Hardrock with voters who thought they were
given the wrong ballot, indicating that was not the case, that it was due to the
address they put on their voter registration.  He stated that the County also
coordinated with the Navajo Nation Human Rights Commission on the matter prior
to the meeting and that they were receptive of what the county has been doing to
address the concerns of the former Supervisor and the Hardrock Chapter President.

He thanked staff and Recorder Michael Sample for being present. He stated that he
is looking forward to attending the Navajo County Fair and that he hopes to see
everyone there.  
Supervisor Whitesinger stated that she is a member of the First Things First
Regional Council, where she served as Vice-Chair, and spoke about how they have
been able to provide funding for the Reach Out and Read, which provides books to
local practitioners who serve children from 0 to 5, indicating that she is grated for the
work they have done there.  She spoke about serving on the American Indian
Structural Heart Diseases Partnership Council, noting that one of the leading causes
of death within American Indian communities is heart disease.  She stated that they
have been working to educate communities on the importance of getting heart
examines and also have been providing training to several medical practitioners
within the communities to be able to provide the services, noting that September 16
-18, 2024 they will have their first Echo screening event open to all community
members. She thanked the Town of Pinetop-Lakeside who posted over 100 flags
within the community, working with the Fishers with Men and the Blue Ridge High
School Football team in remembrance of 9-11 and indicated that we should
recognize all those that serve in different capacities of public service and military.
 She stated that National Day of Service is September 11, 2024, and indicated that
we should find an opportunity for how we can serve your communities through a
small act of kindness. She thanked staff for working on the survey for the election
poll workers and thanked those who have taken the opportunity to respond to the
survey. She shared her appreciation to those that participate and volunteer for the
County Fair.
Vice-Chair Benally thanked staff for the meeting held in Hardrock and the outreach
done there and indicated that we need to do more outreach in the bordering
chapters of Pinon, Whippoorwill and Forest Lake. She indicated that communities
are making comments about receiving the wrong ballot but that it’s because it is
based on the location that they put down on their voter registration. She reported on
attending the North County Healthcare and Winslow City Hall ribbon cuttings.  She
indicated that she attended the NDOT & ADOT session to discuss road conditions
due to the monsoons.  She indicated that BIA has helped clear those roads that
were washed out to make them passable.  She indicated that there is still a drought
in some areas, that one of the chapters is still having water issues but they were
able to repair one of their windmills and shared her appreciation to BIA for helping
repair it. She stated that she has been attending the Chapter Meetings in District I
and that she will be doing a quarterly report at the City of Winslow Council Meeting
later this month.
Chairman Whiting remained everyone about the Navajo County Fair this week and
thanked all those who volunteer.  He indicated that residents will be able to show
case their animals and projects from the past year.  He stated that it’s a great time to
be able to be with our neighbors and have a good time.
 
CALL TO THE PUBLIC:
Belinda Quog, Show Low, spoke about the requests from the public for transparency
and accountability in the election process, indicating that the lack of urgency on
security issues is troubling.  She stated that they have asked for a security
assessment of the voting machines by a highly qualified well-known independent
expert, but that it is meet with excuses.  She indicated that the people of Navajo
County need to know the truth, that the county refused to make things transparent
by having a security assessment of the voting machines.  She stated that if they
                                                                    7 of 11

were allowed to do an assessment, at their own expense, and no issues were found,
they would apologize and let the world know that elections are safe and secure but
that without an assessment they believe that the machines are faulty and can’t be
trusted for a secure election.  She indicated in the past election over one hundred
voters were turned away from vote centers, which demonstrates the county’s lack of
care and security in the election process.  She stated that humans don’t have to turn
off the over and under vote and inquired how many voters were disenfranchised by
it.
Donna Shurwin, Pinetop, thanked Bryan Layton for sending out the survey to the
poll workers, noting that she appreciates that they were sent out and that she hopes
it will continue.  She spoke about an article in the Show Low newspaper regarding
the primary election not being smooth.  She spoke about the inspectors reports for
the 39 vote centers that she requested through a public records request, indicating
that out of the 39 centers 3 didn’t turn one in and that several of 36 that were
completed had no signatures on them.  She inquired how an election can be
certified without an inspectors report. She spoke about a ballot that she found with a
check mark on it during the hand count audit. She stated that when ballots are
adjudicated there is supposed to be a person from each party and a third person,
but that it didn’t happen.  She stated that the primary election should not have been
certified because the chain of custody was not honored.   
Liz Alden, Lakeside, spoke about her experience of being a poll worker, indicating
that she attended the class at a Town Hall, was assigned to Cibecue for last election
and that she went there with another Republican lady.  She indicated that the
Inspector was hostile and that the training that was provided was poor.  She spoke
of concerns that she had after being told that the Apache’s vote Democrat and with
the voters that came in speaking in their native language.  She stated that she left
feeling bad and that she wouldn’t be a poll worker again.  
Celia Lauglin, Overgaard, stated that they feel that power and control was taken
away from the people when elections were centralized and the DS200’s were moved
away from the precincts to the central counters rather than allow a hand count at
the precinct level.  She spoke about Suzanne Spalding with CISA teaching the
judiciary branch and bar association lawyers on how to dismiss or reject anything
challenging elections.  She indicated that Title 16 prevents and protects from this. 
She stated that they haven’t done a good job protecting elections, but that they have
told the Board that state statutes give them the ability and power to stand by the
people and do hand count.  She spoke about an email that was sent in 2020 to the
Department of Justice from Bryan Watson, regarding a meeting he was invited to in
Pima County were a presentation was given on regarding plans to embed 35,000
votes in a spread configured distribution to each democrat candidates vote totals,
that the distribution would be embedded across the total registered voter range and
would not exceed the registered voter count and that all embedded votes would not
be found if audited because the embeds are in groups of ten.     
Michael Pitts, Overgaard, demanded that use of all machine systems be stopped,
along with other programs, and machines which are interfering with the electoral
process.  He stated that the current electoral process has been interfering in the
people election.  He indicated that all elections shall be free and equal and no
power, civil or military shall interfere to prevent the free exercise of the right of
suffrage.   He spoke about the courts having laws that affirm the rights secured by
the constitution, and that the government officials have been given multiple notices
and affidavits informing them that the people’s rights are being trespassed.  He
spoke about the counties entering into unconstitutional contracts with corporations
                                                                    8 of 11

<!-- page 16 -->
that own and operate the systems, machines or devices, which have made the
people unable to observe the electoral chain of custody for ballots.  He indicated that
this is an interference with the Arizona constitution required for free to equal
elections and that is has disenfranchised people.  He requested a hand count for the
2024 election.
Jerry Hubbard, Overgaard, spoke about the ballot drop boxes used in the Primary
Election, indicating that he attended a meeting with Bryan Layton regarding the drop
boxes around county having video cameras installed.  He stated that there are 17
drop boxes and that he is not aware of any of them having video monitoring in past
even though the election procedures manual specifying that they must be monitored
by county employees.  He stated that the video monitoring was at least a step in
right direction and better than no monitoring at all.  He indicated he was pleased that
two weeks prior to the election a camera was installed on the drop box at his polling
location.  He stated that after viewing some of videos provided by the county it was
obvious that the camera system on ballot drop boxes is still few and far between. 
He stated that he was a poll worker for the Primary Election with the position of
Marshall he assisted the county employee in unloading and counting the ballots
from the drop box at the close of election and signed the tally sheet to corroborate
the ballot count as required by the election procedures manual to maintain the chain
of custody.  He stated that the FOIA request was made for the images of the tally
sheets and that the video and only two images of tally sheets were received.  He
inquired where all the other tally sheets images were that should have been
included and how the request could be ignored for the documents and the video
showing proper chain of custody and security for the mail in ballots.  He inquired
when cameras will be installed on all of the 17 drop boxes for the general election.
Roger Gorres, Heber-Overgaard, inquired if there is a policy in place for Navajo
County employees to respond to correspondence and where he can find the policy
to know when he will receive a reply.  He inquired as to the policy of restricting a poll
worker to observer only one location on election day and requested that it be
changed to allow them to observer when, where and how many locations they want
without notice.  He thanked the Navajo County Public Works Director for being at the
Sheriff’s Office public meeting in Overgaard and for replacing the small flashing sign
to larger flashing sign on 277 so drivers can see it and reduce the amount of jake
brake operations that happen while he’s sleeping. 
 
ADJOURN: 10:25 a.m. meeting adjourned. 
 
WORK SESSION:
At 10:55 a.m. All Supervisors were present with staff.
 
Madhav Mundle discussed the implementation of a Payment Management Program
to address the significant financial constraints in maintaining 720 miles of roadways.
The program, developed by consultants, uses data-driven analytics to prioritize road
maintenance, focusing on cost-effective strategies like slurry seals and chip seals.
The program aims to maximize the use of the $2.5 million annual budget, with a
10-year plan to prioritize road segments. Supervisor Whiting emphasized the need
for a county-wide approach to road maintenance, while Fern Benally highlighted the
challenges of maintaining roads in Navajo areas and requested better
communication and support from Public Works. Mr. Mundle also covered the
development of a Construction and Design Standards and Specification Manual to
ensure consistent and safe construction practices.
                                                                    9 of 11 
DRAFT

<!-- page 17 -->
Mischa Larish discussed the permit and code enforcement service enhancements.
The previous permitting system was a paper-based, in-person process with limited
management tools. The new online citizen portal provides a digital, web-based
process with improved tracking and management capabilities. The citizen portal
allows users to submit building permits, access county road information, report code
violations, and schedule inspections online 24/7. This streamlines the process and
provides better transparency. The new permitting system includes comprehensive
workflow tracking and reporting tools that allow staff to better manage and prioritize
inspections and plan reviews. This has helped reduce review times. The code
enforcement process has been enhanced with the addition of a hearing officer. This
has provided more tools to address code violations, with 64 citations issued so far.
The hearing officer process has resulted in 15 properties coming into compliance
and 18 working towards compliance. The new systems and processes have
improved customer service, transparency, and efficiency in the permitting and code
enforcement functions. Staff highlighted the importance of encouraging residents to
utilize the online citizen portal to take advantage of these enhancements.
Supervisor Seymore expressed strong support for the new permitting system and
code enforcement enhancements. He noted that the data-driven, transparent
approach has been well-received by contractors and has helped expedite the
permitting process, which is critical given the seasonal nature of construction in the
region. Supervisor Weisinger appreciated the consistency and equity the new
systems bring, ensuring all residents and developers are treated the same. She
highlighted the importance of clear communication of timelines and expectations,
which the new processes facilitate. Chairman Whiting acknowledged the frustrations
expressed by Supervisor Benally regarding Navajo roads and offered to support her
in advocating for partnerships and funding opportunities at the state and federal
level, if she can provide more information on the specific needs and strategies.
Overall, the Supervisors were very supportive of the improvements made to the
permitting and code enforcement functions. They recognized the value of the
data-driven, transparent approach in providing better service to residents and
developers, while also creating a framework for consistent, equitable
decision-making.
 
Rochelle Lacapa gave a presentation on Guidelines for Successful Transportation
Partnerships. Ms. Lacapa covered Characteristics of Successful Partnership
projects: 1. Budget falls within the $100,000 procurement threshold or is within the
county's approved budget capacity; 2. Clearly defined scope of work, including cost
estimates and documentation; 3. Achievable projects within the procurement
timelines, with projects over $100,000 requiring an earlier start; Eligible
Transportation Partnership Projects: 1. Public roadway projects, including gravel,
culverts, storm drains, roadway signing and striping. 2. Surface
transportation-related projects on public roadways, such as box culverts, low-flow
crossings, and impact attenuation devices; Ineligible Transportation Partnership
Projects: 1. Utility work, private driveways, non-public roadways, assistance for
funeral services, fuel tank purchases, and equipment/materials not exclusively used
for transportation. Ms. Lacapa further described the types of non-construction
partnership projects which can be highly visible projects with immediate impact and
lower visibility projects with long timelines. Ms. Lacapa gave information on grant
opportunities focusing on how the County can support tribal entities as co-applicants
                                                                    10 of 11 
DRAFT

<!-- page 18 -->
for AZ SMART grants by providing letters of support for grant applications to
demonstrate partnership. Lastly, Ms. Lacapa discussed Intergovernmental
Agreements, indicating that all transportation partnership projects require an IGA
with the lead agency, which can include the Navajo Nation Department of
Transportation, tribal transportation departments, ADOT, and incorporated
cities/towns. The presentation emphasized the importance of building strong
relationships with partner entities, clear communication, and aligning projects with
the county's budget and procurement timelines to ensure successful transportation
partnerships.
 
Supervisor Whitesinger expressed that the presentation provided a helpful
framework and template for understanding the process and requirements around
transportation partnerships. She noted that this clarity will be valuable in
communicating with partners, especially when there are changes in elected officials
or staff. Supervisor Seymore commented that the visual aids and information
presented were very useful, as they help establish a common understanding of how
the county can legally and effectively engage in these partnerships. He appreciated
the delineation between high-visibility, immediate impact projects versus
longer-term, lower-visibility initiatives. Chairman Whiting acknowledged the
importance of the relationships and communication emphasized in the presentation.
He offered to be supportive and echo the county's voice when Supervisors are
advocating for partnerships and funding opportunities with state and federal entities,
if provided more information on specific needs and strategies. Overall, the
Supervisors seemed to appreciate the comprehensive framework outlined in the
presentation. They recognized the value in having a clear, standardized approach to
transportation partnerships that balances the county's obligations, budget
constraints, and the need for visible, impactful projects for constituents. The
Supervisors expressed a desire to utilize this framework moving forward in their
discussions and collaborations with partner agencies.
 
The work session adjourned at 1:38 p.m.

 CONTRACT FOR PROFESSIONAL SERVICES 
 
 
AGREEMENT made as of September 24, 2024, between Navajo County ("County") 
and Johnson Walzer Associates, LLC. (“Consultant”). 
 
WHEREAS, County requires certain professional services for Navajo County 
Administration Building Public Service Counters Limited Architectural Services (“Project”); 
and 
 
WHEREAS, Consultant represents that it has the necessary expertise to provide such 
services in accordance with the terms of this Agreement. 
 
NOW, THEREFORE, the parties agree as follows: 
 
1. Services.  Consultant shall perform the work described in the Scope of Work dated 
August 21, 2024, and those additional items set forth in the Consultant’s schedule and 
fee proposal dated August 21, 2024, in a competent and professional manner to the 
satisfaction of County.  The Scope of Work and the Consultant’s schedule and fee 
proposal are attached hereto and by this reference incorporated herein.  If any 
incorporated term is inconsistent with the Agreement, this Agreement shall control.   
 
2. Consultant’s Expertise.  Consultant warrants that it has the ability, authority, 
capacity and professional expertise to perform this Agreement.  Consultant shall 
provide an adequate staff of experienced personnel, capable of and devoted to the 
successful accomplishment of the work to be performed hereunder.  Consultant shall 
assign specific individuals to key positions.  Once assigned to work under this 
Agreement, key personnel shall not be removed or replaced without County’s prior 
written approval.  
 
3. Independent Contractor.  Consultant acknowledges that it is an independent 
contractor.  Neither party is or shall purport to be an agent, employee, partner, joint 
venturer or associate of the other.  An employee or agent of one party shall not be 
deemed an employer or agent of the other party for any purpose.  Taxes or Social 
Security contributions will not be withheld from any payment by County, and 
Consultant shall be solely responsible for such matters.

<!-- page 21 -->
Page -2- 
4. Subcontracts.  Consultant shall not enter into any subcontract with respect to any of 
the work to be performed hereunder without County’s prior written approval.  All 
subcontracts shall comply with applicable federal and state laws and regulations and 
shall impose on the subcontractor substantially the same obligations as are imposed 
on Consultant by this Agreement with respect to those matters covered by Sections 8, 
9, 10, 12, 15 and 18.  Consultant is responsible for full performance of this 
Agreement regardless of whether subcontractors are used. 
 
5. Time for Completion.  Consultant’s services will be provided in a manner consistent 
with the degree of care and skill ordinarily exercised by members of the same 
profession currently practicing under similar circumstances.  Consultant’s services 
shall be performed as expeditiously as is consistent with professional skill and care 
and the orderly progress of the Project, and in accordance with an agreed upon 
schedule for the performance of the Services hereunder.  Time limits established by a 
schedule approved by County shall not, except for reasonable cause, be exceeded by 
County or Consultant.  The Schedule shall be adjusted, if necessary, as the Project 
proceeds.  The date for final performance shall be extended by the number of days 
that performance is delayed by governmental approval or review procedures or other 
causes beyond Consultant’s reasonable control, as jointly confirmed in writing by the 
parties’ representatives. 
 
6. Payment.  County shall pay to Consultant a not-to-exceed sum of seven thousand 
five hundred dollars ($7,500.00) as payment in full for all services rendered by 
Consultant pursuant to this Agreement.  Payments shall be made within 30 days of 
County’s receipt of Consultant’s monthly invoices.  Each invoice shall detail the work 
performed during the billing period.  Invoice amounts in percent of Project cost shall 
not exceed the percentage of completion of the Project as approved by County.   
 
7. Defects in Work.  County may reject any work product that fails to meet customary 
professional standards or Project specifications.  Consultant agrees to promptly 
remedy all such deficiencies.  The parties shall make a good faith effort to resolve any 
controversy or claim through informal negotiation as set forth in Section 12.  No 
compensation shall be paid for any rejected work until such issues have been 
resolved. 
 
8. Insurance Requirements.   
 
A. Consultant shall maintain in effect, at all times during the term of this 
Agreement, insurance adequate to protect County and its agents, 
representatives, officers, officials and employees against such losses as set 
forth below.  Consultant shall provide County with a current Certificate of 
Insurance or a certified copy of the insurance policy naming County as an 
additional insured (except for Errors and Omissions / Professional Liability 
coverage). 
 
 
B. The following types and amounts of insurance are required as minimums:

<!-- page 22 -->
Page -3- 
  
Worker's Compensation    Statutory 
Professional Liability    $1,000,000 each occurrence 
           and annual aggregate 
Consultant’s Protective Bodily Injury  $1,000,000 each occurrence  
           and annual aggregate 
Consultant’s Protective Personal Property $1,000,000 each occurrence  
           and annual aggregate 
Automobile Bodily Injury and Property Damage $1,000,000 each 
occurrence 
       and annual aggregate 
Valuable Papers     $100,000 
 
Standard minimum deductibles are allowed.  Any deductibles are the 
responsibility of Consultant.  Consultant shall immediately inform County of 
any cancellation of insurance or any decrease in the amount of coverage at 
least 30 days before such action takes place.  A violation of this provision may 
be treated as a material breach by County.  Consultant shall notify County 
upon any termination of its regular professional liability coverage and shall 
obtain tail coverage for a minimum of five years from the termination date. 
 
C. Consultant acknowledges that the amounts of insurance coverage stated above 
shall not limit its liability under this Agreement. 
 
9. Indemnity.  To the fullest extent allowable by law, Consultant shall defend, 
indemnify and hold harmless County and its representatives, officers, officials and 
employees (the “Indemnitees”) from and against any and all claims, damages, losses 
and expenses (including but not limited to attorney fees, court costs, and the costs of 
appellate proceedings) relating to, arising out of or resulting from Consultant’s 
negligent acts, errors, mistakes or omissions in the performance of this Agreement. 
Consultant’s duty to defend, hold harmless and indemnify the Indemnitees shall arise 
in connection with any claim, damage, loss or expense that is attributable to bodily 
injury, sickness, disease, death or injury to, impairment or destruction of property 
including loss of use resulting therefrom, caused in whole or in part by any negligent 
act, error, mistake or omission in the performance of this Agreement (including those 
by any person for whose negligent acts, errors, mistakes or omissions Consultant may 
be liable) to the extent of Consultant’s negligence or fault.  The amount and type of 
insurance coverage requirements set forth above shall not limit the scope of the 
indemnity in this paragraph. 
 
10. Records.  Consultant shall retain, and shall require each subcontractor to retain, all 
books, accounts, reports, files and other records relating to this Agreement for a 
period of five years after completion of the work.  All such documents shall be 
subject to inspection and audit by County upon reasonable notice during normal 
business hours.  A legible copy of any or all such documents shall be produced by 
Consultant at the request and expense of County.

<!-- page 23 -->
Page -4- 
11. Non-Assignment.  Consultant shall not assign any right or interest in this Agreement 
without County’s prior written approval, nor shall Consultant delegate any duty 
hereunder without County’s prior written approval.   
 
12. Negotiation of Disputes.  The parties shall make a good faith effort to resolve any 
claim or controversy or claim through informal negotiation.  Notice of any claim or 
controversy shall be provided in writing, with supporting documentation, to the 
recipient designated in Section 21.  The recipient shall have seven calendar days to 
prepare and deliver a written response.  If the parties fail to resolve the disputes 
within a reasonable period of not less than ten days, either party may pursue available 
legal remedies. 
 
13. Suspension and Termination by County: 
 
A. Suspension.  County may, without cause, order Consultant in writing to suspend, 
delay or interrupt its performance in whole or part.  An adjustment shall be made 
to the completion date and for any increase in the cost of performance resulting 
from the suspension, delay or interruption.  No adjustment shall be made to the 
extent that performance is or would have been suspended, delayed or interrupted 
by another cause for which Consultant is responsible. 
 
B. Termination for Cause.  County may terminate this Agreement for cause if 
Consultant refuses or fails to supply enough properly skilled workers to perform 
this Agreement; fails to make required payments to subcontractors; disregards 
laws, ordinances, rules, regulations or orders of a public authority having 
jurisdiction; or otherwise commits a material breach of this Agreement.  When 
any of the foregoing causes exist, County shall give Consultant written notice and 
a ten-day opportunity to cure the default.  If the default remains uncured, County 
may (without prejudice to any other rights or remedies it may have) terminate this 
Agreement and finish the work or cause it to be finished.  If the unpaid balance of 
the Agreement exceeds the cost of finishing the work (including any expenses 
incurred by County as a result of Consultant’s default), Consultant shall be 
entitled to payment for its performance up to the amount of such excess.  If the 
cost of finishing the work exceeds the unpaid balance, Consultant shall pay the 
difference to County. 
 
C. Termination for Convenience.  County, by written notice to Consultant, may 
terminate this Agreement in whole or part when in the sole discretion of County it 
is in County’s best interests to do so.  Consultant shall be paid for all material, 
equipment and services provided, as well as reasonable termination expenses and 
a reasonable allowance for profit and overhead, provided that such payments, 
exclusive of termination expenses, shall not exceed the total amount payable 
pursuant to Section 6 less any payments previously made to Consultant.  
Consultant shall not be entitled to profit and overhead on material, equipment and 
services which were not provided. 
 
D. Consultant’s Responsibilities.  Upon receipt of a termination notice, Consultant

<!-- page 24 -->
Page -5- 
shall (a) promptly discontinue all services (unless the notice directs otherwise), 
and (b) deliver or otherwise make available to County copies of all data, design 
calculations, drawings, specifications, reports, estimates, summaries and other 
information and materials developed or accumulated by Consultant in performing 
this Agreement. 
 
14. Termination by Consultant.  Consultant may terminate this Agreement upon written 
notice to County if performance is made impossible for a period of 30 consecutive 
calendar days for any of the following reasons through no act or fault of Consultant or 
its agents, employees or subcontractors: issuance of an order by a court or other 
public authority having jurisdiction; an act of government, such as a declaration of 
national emergency; or a natural disaster or other Act of God.  Consultant may also 
terminate this Agreement upon written notice and a ten-day opportunity to cure if 
County fails to make any payment within the time set forth in Section 6.  Consultant 
may also terminate this Agreement upon written notice if suspensions, delays or 
interruptions by County equal in the aggregate more than 100% of the total number of 
days scheduled for completion. 
 
15. Governing Law.  This Agreement shall be governed by the law of the State of 
Arizona.  Any suit arising out of this Agreement shall be brought in the state courts of 
Arizona (with venue in Navajo County) or the federal District of Arizona, but only 
after informal negotiation pursuant to Section 12. 
 
16. Incorporation of Applicable Laws.  Every provision of law required by statute or 
regulation to be in this Agreement will be read and enforced as though included 
herein.  Each party shall promptly notify the other upon discovery that any such 
provision has been omitted. 
 
17. Non-Collusion.  The provisions of A.R.S. § 38-511 are incorporated herein by this 
reference. 
 
18. One-Year Limitation on Actions.  No action shall be maintained by Consultant on 
any claim based upon or arising out of this Agreement unless such action is 
commenced within one year after County’s final payment hereunder. 
 
19. Term. This Contract for Professional Service is valid for one (1) year from the date 
the agreement was made between the two parties. Upon mutual agreement between 
the parties, this contract may be renewed up to four (4) additional one-year terms. 
 
20. Entire Agreement.  This Agreement and those documents incorporated by reference 
represent the entire understanding between the parties.  No amendment shall be 
effective unless executed by both parties with the same formality as this Agreement.  
Provided, however, that the County’s project manager is authorized to modify the 
Scope of Work, in writing, with the concurrence of Consultant so long as the project 
is not changed substantially or significant additional compensation is not required. 
 
21. Severability.  The provisions of this Agreement are severable.  Any provision held to

provision.

Re:     Navajo County Administration Building Public Service Counters 
 Limited Architectural Services 
 
Dear Grant, 
Thanks for showing me around the existing Navajo County offices in Holbrook .  Johnson Walzer Associates 
would be pleased to provide limited  architectural services for the public service counters project at the exis ting 
administration building near Holbrook. 
 
PROJECT UNDERSTANDING.    Public access to the administration building is being returned to the original main 
entrance.  As a result, employee security is to be provided at the serv ice interface with th e public.  The general 
strategy is to create  a wall between the staff and public, with secure o penings for the use of the Treasurer, 
Assessor and Recorder. The existing workstations are to be maintained to the greatest extent possible. The wall 
and service op enings are plan ned to be constructed by County personnel, depending on the Building Official 
requirements.  The plans are to be approved by both the Navajo County Building Department and the State Fire 
Marshal. 
 
ARCHITECTURAL SERVICES. We plan the following tasks: 
1. Gather any available existing construction documents and current AutoCAD files for the existing 
building.  
2. Review for code compliance. This will include studying current  occupant load and exiting requirements. 
This will guide exit requirements through the planned security wall. 
3. Review exis ting mechanical plans for air flow issues asso ciated with the  planned new wall.  No 
mechanical engineering is included in this proposal. 
4. Review existing electrical p lans to identify general p ower and lighting issues asso ciated with the new 
wall.  No electrical engineering is included in this proposal. 
5. Confirm the space requirements for the service wind ows, using programming information provided by 
the County.

<!-- page 27 -->
6. Prepare a base floor plan, incorporating the provided AutoCAD drawing files o f the  existing building.  
These may not include actual conditions, which will need to be field verified by the County construction 
team. 
7. Prepare a schematic architectural floor plan, reflected ceiling plan interior elevation and wall section, for 
County review and comment.  This will show the new wall, counter concept and exits.  It will not show in 
detail cubicles behind the wall. 
8. Revise plans as needed and provide dimensions for wall layout. 
9. This is a limited scope architectural project and is intended to provide the bare min imum to achieve a 
building permit and submittal for the State Fire Marshal.   
10. If the fire marshal nee ds additional documentation or engineering, these services can be provided as 
Additional Services. 
11. Attend any video zoom meetings as needed. 
 
OWNER PROVIDED ITEMS.  You will provide: 
• Any documents that may be helpful related to this project.   
• A single point of contact for communication with the Navajo County team. 
 
SCHEDULE.  We can start work immediately upon your authorization and will work to meet your schedule 
expectations. 
 
FEES.  We will provide the above initial Phase 1 services for a total fixed fee of $7,500.    This is based on roughly 
24 architectural hours and 32 technical hours.   
 
ADDITIONAL SERVICES.  Engineering services or more comprehensive architectural services can be added upon 
request. 
 
ACCEPTANCE.  We will start the work promptly upon your acceptance of this proposal. Please let me know if you 
have any questions. Thank you for this opportunity to continue working together. 
Sincerely,    
 
 
Tim Whiteside, Partner 
 
Accepted: __________________________________________    Date: ________________

between ___________________________________ [NAME OF COVERED ENTITY], a _____  
 _____________ [TYPE OF ORGANIZATION] organized under the laws of ____ [STATE]

WHEREAS, the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services issued regulations on 
“Standards for Privacy of Individually Identifiable Health Information” comprising 45 C.F.R. 
Parts 160 and 164, Subparts A and E (the “Privacy Standards ”), “Security Standards for the 
Protection of Electronic Protected Health Information” comprising 45 C.F.R. Parts 160 and 164, 
Subpart C (the “Security Standards ”), “Standards for Notification in the Case of Breach of 
Unsecured Protected Health Information” comprising 45 C.F.R. Parts 160 and 164, Subpart D (the 
“Breach Notification Standards ”), and “Rules for Compliance and Investigations, Impositions 
of Civil Monetary Penalties, and Procedures for Hearings” comprising 45 C.F.R. Part 160, 
Subparts C, D, and E (“the “Enforcement Rule”) promulgated pursuant to the Health Insurance 
Portability and Accountability Act of 1996 (“HIPAA”), the Health Information Technology for 
Economic and Clinical Health Act (“HITECH Act ”), and the Genetic Information and 
Nondiscrimination Act of 2008 (“GINA”) (the Privacy Standards, the Security Standards, the 
Breach Notification Standards, and the Enforcement Rule are collectively referred to herein as the 
“HIPAA Standards”).
WHEREAS, in conformity with the HIPAA Standards, Business Associate has, and/or will 
create, receive, maintain, or transmit certain Protected Health Information (“PHI”) pursuant
to the services provided or where the provision services involves the disclosure of PHI. 
WHEREAS, Covered Entity is required by the HIPAA Standards to obtain satisfactory 
assurances that Business Associate will appropriately safeguard all PHI created, received, 
maintained, or transmitted by Business Associate on behalf of Covered Entity.
WHEREAS, the parties hereto desire to enter into this Agreement to memorialize their
obligations with respect to PHI pursuant to the requirements of the HIPAA Standards. 
NOW, THEREFORE, Covered Entity and Business Associate agree as follows:
Section 1. Definitions. Except as otherwise specified herein, capitalized terms used, but not defined 
in this Agreement, shall have the same meaning as those terms in 45 C.F.R. Parts 160, 162, and 
164. 
(a) Breach, as used in Section 2 of this Agreement, means the unauthorized acquisition, 
access, use, or disclosure of PHI which compromises the security or privacy of such 
information. 
(b) C.F.R. means the Code of Federal Regulations.

Business Associate shall identify the date, nature, and scope of the Security

Section 3. Permitted Uses and Disclosures of PHI by Business Associate.  
(a) General Use and Disclosure Provisions.  
Except as otherwise limited in this Agreement, Business Associate may use or 
disclose PHI to perform functions, activities, or services for, or on behalf of, 
Covered Entity pursuant to the Service Agreement between the parties, provided 
that such use or disclosure would not violate the Privacy Standards if done by 
Covered Entity. 
(b) Specific Use and Disclosure Provisions.  
(1) Except as otherwise limited in this Agreement, Business Associate may use 
PHI for the proper management and administration of Business Associate, 
or to carry out the legal responsibilities of Business Associate. Except as 
otherwise limited in this Agreement, Business Associate may disclose PHI 
(i) for the proper management and administration of Business Associate, or 
( ) to carry out Business Associate’s legal responsibilities if (a) the disclosure 
is Required by Law, or (b) Business Associate obtains reasonable assurances 
from the person to whom the information is disclosed that the information will 
remain confidential and used or further disclosed only as Required by Law or 
for the purpose for which it was disclosed to the person, and the person notifies 
Business Associate of any instances of which it is aware in which the 
confidentiality of the information has been breached. 
(2) Except as otherwise limited in this Agreement, Business Associate may use 
PHI to provide Data Aggregation services to Covered Entity as permitted 
by 45 C.F.R. § 164.504(e)(2)(i)(B). 
(3) Further, Business Associate may de-identify any and all PHI created or 
received under this Agreement in accordance with 45 C.F.R. § 164.514(b) 
and use such de-identified data in any manner determined by Business 
Associate. 
Section 4. Covered Entity’s Obligations.  
(a) Notice of Change in Privacy Practices. To the extent known to Covered Entity, 
Covered Entity shall notify Business Associate of any limitation(s) in Covered Entity’s 
notice of privacy practices in accordance with 45 C.F.R. §164.520, to the extent that 
such limitation may affect Business Associate’s use or disclosure of PHI. 
(b) Notice of Change in Permissions. To the extent known to Covered Entity, Covered 
Entity shall notify Business Associate of any changes in, or revocation of, 
permission by an Individual to use or disclose PHI, to the extent that such changes 
may affect Business Associate’s use or disclosure of PHI. 
(c) Notice of Change in Use. To the extent known to Covered Entity, Covered Entity 
shall notify Business Associate of any restriction on the use or disclosure of PHI

Section 5. Term and Termination. 
(a) Term. The provisions of this Agreement shall commence on the Effective Date and, 
unless earlier terminated in accordance with the provisions of this Section, shall 
terminate when all of the PHI provided by Covered Entity to Business Associate, or 
created or received by Business Associate on behalf of Covered Entity, is destroyed 
or returned to Covered Entity. 
(b) Termination for Cause. Without limiting the termination rights of the parties 
pursuant to this Agreement and upon Covered Entity’s knowledge of a material 
breach of this Agreement by Business Associate, Covered Entity shall either: 
(1) Provide an opportunity for Business Associate to cure the breach or end the 
violation and, if Business Associate does not cure the breach or end the 
violation within the time specified by Covered Entity, terminate this 
Agreement, if feasible; or 
(2) Immediately terminate this Agreement if cure is not possible, if feasible. 
(c) Termination Without Cause. Either party may terminate this Agreement effective 
upon sixty (60) days (fully Insured groups) / ninety (90) days (self-funded groups) 
advance written notice to the other party. 
(d) Effect of Termination.  
(1) Except as provided in paragraph (2) of this Section 5(d), upon termination 
of this Agreement for any reason, Business Associate shall promptly return 
or destroy all PHI received from, or created or received by Business 
Associate on behalf of, Covered Entity that Business Associate maintains in 
any form and retain no copies. This provision shall apply to PHI that is in 
the possession of subcontractors or agents of Business Associate. 
(2) In the event that Business Associate reasonably believes that returning or 
destroying the PHI is infeasible, Business Associate shall provide to Covered 
Entity prompt notification of the conditions that make return or destruction 
infeasible. Upon Business Associate’s determination that return or destruction 
of PHI is infeasible; Business Associate shall extend the protections of this 
Agreement to such PHI and limit further uses and disclosures of such PHI to 
those purposes that make the return or destruction infeasible, for so long as 
Business Associate maintains such PHI.

 CONTRACT FOR PROFESSIONAL SERVICES  
 
 
AGREEMENT made as of September 24, 2024, between Navajo County ("County") 
and Kimley-Horn and Associates, Inc. (“Consultant”). 
 
WHEREAS, County requires certain professional services for Pinetop Country Club 
Striping Improvements Project (“Project”); and 
 
WHEREAS, Consultant represents that it has the necessary expertise to provide such 
services in accordance with the terms of this Agreement. 
 
NOW, THEREFORE, the parties agree as follows: 
 
1. Services.  Consultant shall perform the work described in the Scope of Work dated 
August 21, 2024, and those additional items set forth in the Consultant’s schedule and 
fee proposal dated August 21, 2024, in a competent and professional manner to the 
satisfaction of County.  The Scope of Work  and the Consultant’s schedule and fee 
proposal are attached hereto and by this reference incorporat ed herein.  If any 
incorporated term is inconsistent with the Agreement, this Agreement shall control.   
 
2. Consultant’s Expertise.  Consultant warrants that it has the ability, authority, 
capacity and professional expertise to perfo rm this Agreement.  Consultant shall 
provide an adequate staff of experienced  personnel, capable of and devoted to the 
successful accomplishment of the work to be performed hereunder.  Consultant shall 
assign specific individuals to key positio ns.  Once assigned to work under this 
Agreement, key personnel shall not be removed or replaced without County’s prior 
written approval.  
 
3. Independent Contractor.  Consultant acknowledges that  it is an independent 
contractor.  Neither party is or shall purport to be an agent, employee, partner, joint 
venturer or associate of the other.  An employee or agen t of one party shall not be 
deemed an employer or agent  of the other party for any purpose.  Taxes or Social 
Security contributions will not be with held from any payment by County, and 
Consultant shall be solely responsible for such matters.

<!-- page 37 -->
Page -2- 
4. Subcontracts.  Consultant shall not enter into any subcontract with respect to any of 
the work to be performed hereunder without County’s prior written approval.  All 
subcontracts shall comply with applicable federal and state laws and regulations and 
shall impose on the subcontractor substantially the same obligations as are imposed 
on Consultant by this Agreement with respect to those matters covered by Sections 8, 
9, 10, 12, 15 and 18.  Consultant is  responsible for full performance of this 
Agreement regardless of whether subcontractors are used. 
 
5. Time for Completion.  Consultant’s services will be provided in a manner consistent 
with the degree of care and skill ordi narily exercised by me mbers of the same 
profession currently practicing under similar circumstances.  Consultant’s services 
shall be performed as expeditiously as is consistent with professional skill and care 
and the orderly progress of the Project, and in accord ance with an agreed upon 
schedule for the performance of the Services hereunder.  Time limits established by a 
schedule approved by County shall not, except for reasonable cause, be exceeded by 
County or Consultant.  The Schedule shall be adjusted, if  necessary, as the Project 
proceeds.  The date for final performance shall be extended by the number of days 
that performance is delayed by governmental approval or review procedures or other 
causes beyond Consultant’s reasonable control, as jointly confirmed in writing by the 
parties’ representatives. 
 
6. Payment.  County shall pay to Consultant a not-to-exceed sum of nineteen thousand 
eight hundred twenty five dollars ($19,825.00 ) as payment in full for all services 
rendered by Consultant pursuant to this Agreement.  Payments shall be made within 
30 days of County’s receipt of Consultant’s monthly invoices.  Each invoice shall 
detail the work performed during the billing period.  Invoice amounts in percent of 
Project cost shall not exceed the percentage of completion of the Project as approved 
by County.   
 
7. Defects in Work.  County may reject any work product that fails to meet customary 
professional standards or Project specifi cations.  Consultant agrees to promptly 
remedy all such deficiencies.  The parties shall make a good faith effort to resolve any 
controversy or claim through informal nego tiation as set forth in Section 12.  No 
compensation shall be paid for any reject ed work until such issues have been 
resolved. 
 
8. Insurance Requirements.   
 
A. Consultant shall maintain in effect , at all times during the term of this 
Agreement, insurance adequate to protect County and its agents, 
representatives, officers, officials and employees against such losses as set 
forth below.  Consultant shall provide County with a current Certificate of 
Insurance or a certified copy of the insurance policy naming County as an 
additional insured (except for Errors and Omissions / Professional Liability 
coverage).

SCOPE OF SERVICES 
 
TASK 1 PROJECT MANAGEMENT AND MEETINGS 
 
1.A. Project Management  
Kimley-Horn will establish a project management sys tem which will provide adequate schedule and 
budget control and will be responsive to input from the County. Project management include managing 
project schedule, coordination, and contracts with our subconsultants, invoicing, and QA/QC. 
 
1.B. Project Meetings 
Kimley-Horn will attend up to two (2) meetings. The  meetings will be held virtually using Microsoft 
Teams and will include up to two (2) Kimley-Horn staff. Anticipated meetings are described as follows: 
 
i Project Kick-off Meeting - which will consist of overview of the scope of work, coordination 
of collection of data, communication plan, and coor dination with project team on technical 
concerns prior to starting work. 
ii Pinetop Country Club Transverse Pavement Marking Pr e-Final Results Meeting 
 
Kimley-Horn will prepare an agenda, sign in sheet, and meeting notes for each meeting. 
 
TASK NO. 2 – DATA COLLECTION & FIELD REVIEW 
The County will furnish the latest as-builts and quarter section maps for County-owned facilities (ROW, 
and easements) that are available for the project limits to Kimley-Horn. 
Kimley-Horn will conduct a field investigation of t he existing conditions and inventory existing 
signing/pavement markings. 
 
TASK NO. 3 – TOPOGRAPHIC SURVEY AND AERIAL MAPPING  
 
Kimley-Horn will obtain aerial photography acquired  from Near Maps/County Assessors website to 
develop a project base map.  
 
Topographic survey is not anticipated for this project.  
 
Kimley-Horn will utilize County as-builts/quarter s ection maps received from the County and Navajo 
County Assessors for ROW and easements to develop a  ROW CADD base file.  
 
TASK NO. 4 - TRAFFIC SIGNING AND PAVEMENT MARKING D ESIGN AND PLANS 
Kimley-Horn will prepare signing and pavement marki ng plans in accordance with Navajo County 
standards and details and the Manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices (MUTCD).  
Kimley-Horn’s design will include the striping of t he new transverse pavement markings at the five (5)  
locations recommended in the Pinetop Country Club S peed Study.

<!-- page 44 -->
Page 3 
 
 
kimley-horn.com  1001 W. Southern Ave, Suite 131, Mesa, AZ 85210 480-207-2666 
 
Kimley-Horn will prepare Pre-Final and Final traffi c signing and pavement marking plan sheets at 1” = 
40’ scale. A total of 14 sheets will be prepared and consist of the following: 
 
• Cover Sheet (1 Sheet) 
• Traffic Signing and Pavement Marking Notes and Leg end Sheet (1 Sheet) 
• Geometric Control Sheets (2 Sheets) 
• Buck Springs Road Signing and Pavement Marking Lay out (10 Sheet) 
 
Kimley-Horn will prepare a list of anticipated quan tities to be submitted at both Pre-Final and Final 
design stage. Quantities will be presented on the plan sheets 
 
Kimley-Horn will provide final deliverables to Coun ty staff in PDF format. Electronic files, including  
CADD files, will be provided for County records. 
 
TASK NO. 5 – REVIEWS AND SUBMITTALS 
 
Kimley-Horn will prepare and submit construction documents at two stages of development: 
• Pre-Final Submittal 
• Final Sealed Submittal 
 
Kimley-Horn will compile comments received from the County following the Pre-Final Submittal. These 
comments will be compiled in a spreadsheet that contains responses and resolutions to each comment. 
This spreadsheet will be submitted at the following submittal stage. This task provides time for internal 
QA/QC reviews before each submittal.  
 
Exclusions

considered additional services. This may include, but is not limited to the following: 
 
• Additional meetings 
• Survey 
• Project Specifications 
• Construction Phasing Plans 
• Traffic Control Plans 
• Post Design Services 
• Observation of Construction  
• Additional submittals 
• Any additional items not mentioned and specificall y listed as a task in the sections above.

Re:     White Mountain Community Center Roofing Replacement 
 A rchitectural Services for Navajo County 
 
Dear Grant, 
Johnson Walzer Associates would be pleased to provide architectural services for the White Mountain  
Community Center Roofing Replacement project at 1594 Johnson Drive in Lakeside, Arizona. 
 
PROJECT UNDERSTANDING.    This project is funded by a federal community development block grant which will 
be administered locally by NACOG. Navajo County will be providing project management.  The project will 
involve roofing material removal and replacement at the existing White Mountain  Community Center, which 
includes a senior center and thrift shop.  The building is wood frame with sloped roofs  and appears to include 
three additions and a front porch extension.   Multiple layers of shingles are installed, some in fair to poor 
condition. The enclosed portion of the building is roughly 7,800 SF, plus entry and rear porches. 
 
The front and rear porches need structural reinforcement.  The present roofing project scope does not include 
structural work.  This project will include replacement of any failing sections of roof sheathing. 
 
ARCHITECTURAL SERVICES. We plan the following tasks: 
1. Gather any available existing construction documents for the existing building.  
2. Visit the site, to document existing conditions and identify any structural concerns. 
3. Prepare architectural base plans for the roof. 
4. Prepare design for County review. This will include a roof plan and notes. 
5. Incorporate County comments and complete the construction documents. These will include a roof 
demolition plan, proposed plan, typical details and a sheet specification.   Simple structural repairs will 
be suggested. 
6. Coordinate as needed with County or NACOG staff in preparation of bid documents. 
7. Submit documents for the Town of Pinetop Lakeside Building Department. 
ATTACHMENT A

<!-- page 54 -->
8. Attend a pre-bid meeting on site in Lakeside. 
9. Attend a video zoom pre-construction conference. 
10. Attend any other video zoom meetings as needed. 
11. Provide standard construction administration services including review of submittals ; response to 
contractor queries; review of proposed change orders; review of pay applications ; and three site visits, 
one during tear off, second during roofing and a final punch list inspection. 
 
OWNER PROVIDED ITEMS.  You will provide: 
• Any documents that may be helpful related to this project.   
• A single point of contact for communication with the Navajo County/NACOG and user groups. 
 
SCHEDULE.  We can start work immediately upon your authorization and will work to meet your schedule 
expectations.  We understand the urgency to get this work started this fall if possible. 
 
FEES.  We will provide the above services for a total fixed fee of $18,000 as follows: 
 
1. Site visit and preliminary design   $4,500 
2. Construction documents   $7,500 
3. Bidding      $1,500 
4. Construction administration   $4,500 
 
ADDITIONAL SERVICES.  Engineering services are not presently anticipated.  Structural engineering services are 
available and could be added if field conditions arise requiring further analysis.  
 
ACCEPTANCE.  We will start the work promptly upon your acceptance of this proposal. Please let me know if you 
have any questions. Thank you for this opportunity to continue working together. 
Sincerely,    
 
 
Tim Whiteside, Partner

Background:

City Pedestrian Improvements project. As part of the CDBG, the Arizona Department of

requires a new signature.

E-11 Compliance Factor and Environmental Assessment Checklist 
Project Name: Joseph City Pedestrian Improvements Project 
Recipient:       
 
A. 24 CFR §58.5 STATUTES, EXECUTIVE ORDERS & REGULATIONS 
PROJECT NAME and DESCRIPTION – Include all contemplated actions that logically are either geographically or functionally part 
of the project:  
 
The project consists of the construction of new ADA-compliant sidewalks, multi-use paths, and crossings, as well as new curbs 
and gutters, along existing streets in the unincorporated community of Joseph City, AZ. The purpose of the project is to improve 
safety for students and other pedestrians and bicyclists travelling between Joseph City Elementary and Joseph City JR/SR High 
Schools. 
 
DIRECTIONS - Check “A” in the Status Column when the project, by its nature, does not affect the resources under consideration; OR 
check “B” if the project triggers formal compliance consultation with the oversight agency, or requires mitigation.  Compliance 
documentation must contain verifiable source documents and relevant base data.  Attach reviews, consultations, and special 
studies as appropriate. See instructions for additional guidance. 
 
B. Compliance Factors: 
Statutes, Executive Orders, and 
Regulations Listed at 24 CFR §58.5 
Status 
A   /   B 
Compliance Determination and Documentation (letters, phone 
calls, on-site visit dates, maps, websites etc.) MUST BE CITED 
1. Historic Preservation  
(includes archeology & relevant 
Tribes) [36 CFR Part 800] 
 
  A file search (AZSITE) and review of historical topographic maps and aerial 
imagery revealed one previously recorded cultural resource adjacent to the 
APE to the south – Historic Route 66 [AZ I:15:156 (ASM)]. The proposed 
work will have no effect on this historical structure.  
 
Additionally, due to the heavily developed and disturbed nature of the APE, 
there is a low chance of encountering cultural resources. Navajo County has 
received a concurrence from SHPO and the San Carlos Apache Tribe on its 
finding of No Adverse Effect. No additional responses were received. See 
Appendix for consultation materials. 
2. Floodplain Management 
       [24 CFR 55, Executive Order 11988] 
Flood Disaster Protection Act (Flood 
Insurance) [§58.6(c)] 
 
  The project area is not in a FEMA-designated floodplain and is exempt from 
Executive order 11988 per 24 CFR part 58.34(a)(12). Based on a review of 
FEMA FIRM # 04017C3304E, effective on 9/26/2008, the project is in Zone 
X, an area of minimal flood hazard, and flood insurance is not required for 
properties within the project area. 
3. Wetland Protection 
[Executive Order 11990] 
 
  The proposed work will be conducted within previously developed municipal 
ROW and does not involve new construction as defined in EO 11990. 
According to the USFWS National Wetlands Inventory (NWI, 2023), one 
man-made, semi-permanently flooded feature intersects the project area. 
This ditch is classified as “riverine.” However, contemporary aerial imagery 
does not display surface manifestations of this ditch. A topographic map 
from 1986 labels a portion of the ditch as an “aqueduct”, which could 
indicate the ditch was later relocated or piped.  
 
Based on the existing conditions, the proposed work will occur entirely 
within previously developed areas and will not impact wetlands protected by 
EO 11990.

<!-- page 67 -->
Page 2 of 17 
REV. 8-2019 
 
4. Coastal Zone Management Act 
[Sections 307(c), (d)] 
Coastal Barrier Resources 
Act/Coastal Barrier Improvement Act 
[§58.6(c)] 
  Projects located in HUD Region IX (CA, AZ, NV, HI, Guam) have no coastal 
barrier resources. Likewise, projects located in the State of Arizona have no 
coastal zones and are therefore considered to be in compliance with the 
related laws and regulations. 
5. Sole Source Aquifers 
[40 CFR 149] 
 
  There are two sole source aquifers in the State of Arizona. These aquifers 
are located within the southern portion of the state and are not in proximity 
to the project. Therefore, these important aquifers will not be directly or 
indirectly affected by the work. See Figure 1 for a map of the project area in 
relationship to sole source aquifers. 
6. Endangered Species Act 
[50 CFR 402] 
 
  According to the USFWS Information for Planning and Consultation IPaC) 
database (Project Code: 2024-0057625), there are four threatened, 
endangered, or candidate species with the potential to be affected by 
activities in the project area: Mexican wolf (Canis lupus baileyi, 
endangered), yellow-billed cuckoo (Coccyzus americanus, threatened), 
Little Colorado spinedace (Lepidomeda vittate, threatened), and Monarch 
butterfly (Danaus plexippus, candidate). According to IPaC, there is no 
designated critical habitat within the project area.  
 
Wilson & Company also discussed the project with Arizona Game and Fish 
Department (AZGFD) and confirmed that the project should have little-to-no 
effect on wildlife or plant communities due to its location within existing, 
developed municipal ROW.  
 
Based on these findings, the project will have no effect on threatened, 
endangered, or candidate species. The full IPaC report and Official Species 
List, dated 05/02/2024, is included in the Appendix. 
7. Wild and Scenic Rivers Act 
[Sections 7(b), and (c)] 
 
  Two resources within the State of Arizona, Fossil Creek and Verde River, 
are protected under the Wild and Scenic Rivers Act. The project is not 
located within a mile of these streams, or any other streams listed as scenic, 
wild, or recreational on the Nationwide Rivers Inventory (NRI). Therefore, 
the project will have no effect on protected resources under the Wild and 
Scenic Rivers Act. See Figure 2 for a map of the project area in relationship 
to Wild and Scenic Rivers. 
8. Clean Air Act  
[Sections 176(c), (d), and  
40 CFR 6, 51, 93] 
 
  The project does not include new construction, conversion of land use, or 
substantial rehabilitation of dwelling units. Additionally, Navajo County 
currently has no nonattainment areas listed (ADEQ 2023). Short-term 
fugitive dust generated through construction activities such as excavation of 
unpaved areas, road dust from vehicles, and emissions from internal 
combustion engines have the potential to impact air quality within the 
analysis area and surrounding areas. Navajo County rules and regulations 
pertaining to dust control and permits will be followed to reduce these 
impacts. Navajo County anticipates that any impacts on air quality from 
fugitive dust and intermittent emissions would be negligible, occurring only 
during the construction period. The project will not result in any long-term 
impacts on air quality. 
9. Farmland Protection Policy Act 
[7 CFR 658] 
 
  The project area is not located within arable lands, and the proposed work 
will be confined to existing, developed ROW areas. Therefore, the FLPP Act 
does not apply. 
10. Environmental Justice 
[Executive Order 12898] 
 
  Within Arizona, 12.8% of the population is at or below the poverty level. 
Within Navajo County, 24.8% of the population is at or below the poverty 
level. Within Joseph City, 11.5% of the population is at or below the poverty 
level (U.S. Census Bureau 2020; ACS 2021 5-year census). While there are 
low-income and minority populations within the community of the proposed 
work, no disproportionate impact on any population is anticipated.

11. HUD Environmental Standards    
Noise Abatement and Control 
[24 CFR 51B] 
  The project will result in minor, short-term increases in noise levels during 
construction. The project area is adjacent to residential zones with 
numerous single and multifamily homes, which may be affected by the 
project during construction. The project will not result in any long-term 
impacts on noise levels. 
Explosive & Flammable Operations 
[24 CFR 51C] 
 
  Reviews of contemporary aerial maps (NETROnline 2023) and the 
NEPAssist and ADEQ online mapping tools indicated there are no above 
ground stationary tanks containing explosive, combustible, or flammable 
materials near the project area. Therefore, the project is in compliance with 
24 CFR Part 51 Subpart C. See Figure 3 for a map of Explosive & 
Flammable Operations near the project. 
Hazardous, Toxic or Radioactive 
Materials & Substances 
[24 CFR 58.5(i)(2)] 
 
  No existing hazardous materials sites were identified within the analysis 
area. A review of online regulatory resources (Arizona Department of 
Environmental Quality eMaps and EPA NEPAssist mapping tools) did not 
indicate any regulatory listings of concern within the analysis area. The 
analysis area contains no brownfield sites, declaration of environmental use 
restriction (DEUR) sites, hazardous waste sites, landfills, material recovery 
facilities, superfund sites, or underground storage tanks.  
 
Immediately adjacent to the analysis area, within 130 feet of the project 
area, are one permanently closed, underground storage tank and two 
hazardous waste facilities. The tank and two waste facilities do not pose any 
ongoing environmental hazards and will not be affected by the proposed 
work.  
 
Additionally, the work would not generate hazardous materials, and any 
waste would be collected, stored, and disposed of in accordance with 
applicable state and federal regulations. Therefore, the project will have no 
effect on hazardous materials. See Figure 4 for a map of known Hazardous, 
Toxic or Radioactive Materials & Substances near the project. 
Airport Clear Zones & Accident 
Potential Zones 
[24 CFR 51D] 
 
  The closest airport, Holbrook municipal airport, is more than 10 miles east-
northeast of the project area. Therefore, the project is not subject to 24 CFR 
Part 51 Subpart D. See Figure 5 for a map of the project area in relationship 
to airports.

18. SUMMARY OF FINDINGS AND CONCLUSIONS 
 (Summarize the findings and conclusions from the checklist items 1 thru 31) 
 
Wilson & Company has fully evaluated the project for its potential to affect the resources considered under the compliance 
factors (Statutes, Executive Orders, and Regulations) listed at 24 CFR §58.5. Based on this evaluation, we have determined 
the project will have no impact on any of these resources. Additionally, we have fully assessed the project’s potential to 
impact land development, socioeconomic, community facilities and services, and unique natural features. Based on this 
assessment, we have determined the project will have no adverse impact on these important values. Long-term, the project 
is anticipated to have beneficial effects on some of these values, especially educational facilities and public safety, because 
it will provide safe access to pedestrian facilities along the streets connecting Joseph City Elementary and Joseph City 
JR/SR High Schools.
